Question 1: question_1

❓ Question

How do I request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example4/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/gres.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise4/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise3/run.sbatch

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To request a single GPU in a SLURM submission script on FASRC Cannon, use the `--gpus-per-node` directive in your `#SBATCH` header. Here is a minimal example: ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=gpu_job #SBATCH --output=%x_%j.out #SBATCH --error=%x_%j.err #SBATCH --nodes=1 #SBATCH --gpus-per-node=1 #SBATCH --time=00:30:00 #SBATCH --mem=16G #SBATCH --partition=gpu # Your command here module load cuda nvidia-smi ``` ### Key points: * **`--gpus-per-node=1`**: Requests one GPU on the allocated node [Slurm Workload Manager – sbatch](https://slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html). * **`--partition=gpu`**: Ensures the job is scheduled on a GPU-enabled queue. * **`--nodes=1`**: Typically, you request one node per GPU unless you are doing multi-node distributed training. If you need more than one GPU, simply increase the number (e.g., `--gpus-per-node=4`) [github.com/fasrc/User_Codes](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch). Would you like help with setting up the environment (e.g., loading CUDA modules) or configuring your application to use the GPU?
✅ Expected Answer
Add #SBATCH --gpus=1 to your submission script and SLURM will give the job access to one GPU.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 3: question_3

❓ Question

How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/preempt.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
If you don't specify a memory request, SLURM defaults to **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). That is almost certainly too little for most jobs. If your job exceeds this limit, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. To request more memory, add to your job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the memory in MB (e.g., `--mem 4000` for 4 GB) or use the `G` suffix (e.g., `--mem 4G`) [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). A good practice is to start with a generous request, run your job, and then use `jobstats JOBID` to see how much memory it actually used, then adjust downward for future submissions [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).
✅ Expected Answer
If you omit the memory request you are granted 100 MB by default, which is usually too little and will likely cause the job to be killed for going over.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

🛠️ Tool Call #1: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: How much memory does a SLURM job get on Cannon if I don't specify a memory request?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: fetch_catalog_document
Args: No query found.
🤖 Assistant Message #3
If you don't specify a memory request, SLURM defaults to **100 MB per node** [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). That is almost certainly too little for most jobs. If your job exceeds this limit, it will be killed with an `OUT_OF_MEMORY` error. To request more memory, add to your job script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the memory in MB (e.g., `--mem 4000` for 4 GB) or use the `G` suffix (e.g., `--mem 4G`) [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/). A good practice is to start with a generous request, run your job, and then use `jobstats JOBID` to see how much memory it actually used, then adjust downward for future submissions [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).

❓ Question

What is the storage quota for a FASRC user home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The home directory quota for FASRC users is **100 GB**, with a **95 GB soft quota** that triggers notifications when exceeded [Home directory full](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full/).

Each user gets a 100 GB home directory, enforced as a hard limit.

Question 5: question_5

❓ Question

How much storage does a FASRC lab group directory get, and how many files?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
A FASRC **Lab Directory** (`/n/holylabs/<labname>_lab`) provides: * **Storage:** 4 TB (this quota cannot be expanded) [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) * **Files:** The documentation does not specify a file-count limit for lab directories. Additional notes: * Lab directories are **retention allowed** (no automatic deletion based on age). * They are **not backed up** (no snapshots). [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/) If you need more storage or different features, FASRC also offers **netscratch** (50 TB, 90-day retention, no backup) and paid options like **Compute Storage** and **Lab Storage**. See the [Data Storage Workflow](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/) page for a full overview.
✅ Expected Answer
Each lab gets a 4 TiB lab directory as a hard limit, with a 1 million file limit.

Question 7: question_7

❓ Question

What hostname do I SSH to for command-line access to the FASRC cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
SSH to **`login.rc.fas.harvard.edu`** using your FASRC username. For example: ```bash ssh <username>@login.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` This is the standard login node for the FASRC cluster. If you're accessing the FASSE cluster (for Data Security Level 3 data), the login process is similar but you'll need to follow the FASSE-specific setup steps.
✅ Expected Answer
SSH to login.rc.fas.harvard.edu using your FASRC username, password, and OpenAuth token.

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The command to view your lab’s fairshare information on FASRC is: ```bash fairshare -g ``` This displays the fairshare score for your group/lab. You can also use: - `fairshare -u <username>` to check an individual user’s fairshare - `fairshare -g <groupname>` to check a specific group’s fairshare For more details, see [Fairshare](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).
✅ Expected Answer
Use sshare (e.g. sshare --account=<lab> -a) to see RawShares, NormShares, RawUsage, EffectvUsage, and the FairShare score for an account.
🎯 Expected Source Documents

Question 10: question_10

❓ Question

How long are FASRC home directory snapshots kept?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fas-rc-research-data-retention-and-deletion-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-deletion-recommendations

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The snapshots are stored in a hidden `.snapshot` directory within your home directory. You can access them by navigating directly to that directory (e.g., `cd ~/.snapshot`). Hourly, daily, and weekly snapshots are available within subdirectories named by date. **Important notes:** - If you delete your entire home directory, the snapshots are also deleted and are **not recoverable**. - Home directories have a 100 GB hard limit, and snapshots count toward this quota.
✅ Expected Answer
Home directories have daily snapshots retained for 2 weeks, accessible via the .snapshot directory.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage

🤖 Assistant Message #6
FASRC home directory snapshots are retained for **2 weeks** [Home and Lab directories – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage/). The snapshots are stored in a hidden `.snapshot` directory within your home directory. You can access them by navigating directly to that directory (e.g., `cd ~/.snapshot`). Hourly, daily, and weekly snapshots are available within subdirectories named by date. **Important notes:** - If you delete your entire home directory, the snapshots are also deleted and are **not recoverable**. - Home directories have a 100 GB hard limit, and snapshots count toward this quota.

Question 11: question_11

❓ Question

My SLURM job on Cannon was killed for running out of memory. How do I find out how much memory it actually used, and how do I request more next time?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to find out how much memory your job used and how to request more: ### How much memory did it actually use? 1. **For a completed (successful) job:** Use `sacct` to see the peak memory recorded by Slurm: ```bash sacct -j <JOBID> --format=JobID,MaxRSS ``` Slurm records the true peak memory only for jobs that complete successfully. 2. **For a job that failed with `OUT_OF_MEMORY`:** The memory recorded by Slurm will be inaccurate. Instead, use **`jobstats`** to see the actual memory usage: ```bash jobstats <JOBID> ``` This works for both running and completed jobs and gives you the real memory peak. 3. **For a running job:** You can also check current usage with: ```bash jobstats <JOBID> ``` ### How to request more memory next time Add this line to your SLURM submission script: ```bash #SBATCH --mem X ``` where `X` is the memory in MB. You can also use `G` for GB. For example, to request 4 GB: ```bash #SBATCH --mem 4G ``` ### Tips for choosing the right amount - Start by running your job with `jobstats` to see how much memory it actually uses. - Set `--mem` to something **slightly larger** than what `jobstats` reports, since `--mem` is a hard upper limit. - Requesting smaller memory amounts helps the scheduler find a node more easily. Job slots average about 4000 MB per core, but most jobs need much less. - For multi-node jobs, `--mem` is the maximum memory used on **any one node**, so make sure your tasks are evenly distributed (e.g., using `--ntasks-per-node`). For more details, see [Slurm memory limits – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/).
✅ Expected Answer
After the job ends, run sacct -j <jobid> --format=JobID,Elapsed,ReqMem,MaxRSS to compare the peak memory used (MaxRSS) against what was requested (ReqMem). In your next submit script raise the request with --mem=<N>G (whole job) or --mem-per-cpu=<N>G, setting it a bit above the observed MaxRSS so normal variation doesn't kill the job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Question 13: question_13

❓ Question

How do I find which versions of a software package are available as modules on Cannon, and how should I load it so my workflow keeps working when defaults change?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
To find available versions of a software package on Cannon, use: - **`module avail <package>`** — lists versions available in your current environment (e.g., `module avail matlab`). - **`module --default avail <package>`** — shows all versions regardless of currently loaded modules. - **`module spider <package>`** — provides detailed info, including dependencies and compatibility with different compilers/MPIs. To load a specific version, use the full module name: ```bash module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01 ``` It is **strongly recommended** to always specify the exact version in your scripts and workflows. This protects your jobs from breaking when the default version changes. For example, instead of `module load matlab`, use `module load matlab/R2025b-fasrc01` [Lmod software modules – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro/). If you need to see what modules are currently loaded, run `module list`.
✅ Expected Answer
Search with module spider <name> to list all available modules matching the string regardless of your current environment. Then load with both the name and a specific version (module load <name>/<version>) — FASRC strongly recommends this so you are protected if the default version changes later.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro

Question 14: question_14

❓ Question

What's the recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment for a project on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cursor-remote-development-via-ssh-and-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The recommended way to set up an isolated Python environment on Cannon is to use **Mamba** (a faster drop-in replacement for Conda) inside a **virtual environment**. Direct use of `pip` outside of a virtual environment is discouraged. Here is the recommended workflow: 1. **Start an interactive session** to ensure you have the necessary resources: ```bash salloc --partition test --nodes=1 --cpus-per-task=2 --mem=4GB --time=0-02:00:00 ``` 2. **Load the Python module**, which provides Mamba: ```bash module load python ``` 3. **Create a new environment**. You can create an empty environment or install packages immediately: ```bash # Create an empty environment mamba create -n my_project_env # Or create and install packages at once (recommended for speed) mamba create -n my_project_env python=3.10 numpy pandas ``` 4. **Activate the environment**: ```bash source activate my_project_env ``` 5. **Install additional packages** as needed: ```bash mamba install -y <package_name> ``` **Key Notes:** * **Channels:** The FASRC `python` module uses `conda-forge` as the default channel. The default Anaconda channel (`repo.anaconda.com`) is blocked on the cluster due to licensing restrictions. * **Storage:** By default, environments are stored in `~/.conda/envs`. If you need to store them elsewhere (e.g., a Lab shared directory), you can set the `CONDA_ENVS_PATH` environment variable before creating the environment. * **Job Submission:** When submitting jobs, ensure you load the `python` module and activate your environment within your job script. For more details, see the [Python Package Installation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python-package-installation) guide.
✅ Expected Answer
Load a module that provides mamba (python/3*, Miniforge3, or Mambaforge), then create an isolated environment, e.g. mamba create -n data_env python=3.9 pandas numpy, and activate it with source activate data_env. FASRC recommends using Miniforge with mamba so dependencies are isolated and you avoid conflicts rather than working in the base environment.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 15: question_15

❓ Question

How is my job's priority determined on Cannon, and how can I see the priority of my pending jobs?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/priority_multifactor.html, slurm.schedmd.com/scontrol.html, slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, slurm.schedmd.com/mc_support.html, slurm.schedmd.com/mcs.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on FASRC documentation, here is how job priority works on Cannon and how to view it: ### How Job Priority is Determined Job priority on Cannon is calculated using two main components, managed by Slurm's FairShare system: 1. **FairShare Score:** This is the primary factor. It is based on your lab's "Share" (the portion of the cluster granted to your account) versus the amount of resources your lab has actually used. Users whose labs have not yet used their full resource grant receive a higher FairShare score, which translates to higher job priority. This ensures that labs who haven't used their fair share get priority over those who have overused their allocation. The FairShare score is multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to create an integer priority value [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). 2. **Job Age:** Jobs that have been pending in the queue longer accrue additional priority over time. This helps ensure that older jobs eventually get scheduled even if newer jobs have a slightly higher FairShare score. The scheduler uses Trackable RESources (TRES) to account for CPU, GPU, and memory usage when calculating these scores [Fairshare and Job Accounting](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). ### How to See the Priority of Your Pending Jobs You can view the priority of your pending jobs using the `squeue` command with the `--priority` flag or by customizing the output format to include the priority column. **Option 1: Use the `--priority` flag** ```bash squeue --priority -u <your_username> ``` This will list your pending jobs sorted by priority. For jobs submitted to multiple partitions, this option lists the job once per partition [Slurm squeue documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/squeue.html). **Option 2: Customize output to show priority** You can use the `-o` (or `--format`) option to explicitly display the priority column: ```bash squeue -u <your_username> -o "%.10i %.10P %.8j %.8u %.10T %.10S %.10P %R" ``` * `%P` in the format string corresponds to the job priority. **Option 3: Use `showq-slurm`** FASRC also provides a convenient command to list jobs in priority order: ```bash showq-slurm -o -u -q <partition_name> ``` This lists the priority order of jobs for your user in a specific partition [Convenient Slurm Commands](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands/).
✅ Expected Answer
A job's priority combines two parts: your FairShare score multiplied by a weighting factor (10,000,000) to make it an integer, plus job age, which accrues while the job waits and caps at 3 days on Cannon. Inspect the breakdown for pending jobs with sprio, or use showq -o -p <partition> to see the pending queue ordered by priority.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 16: question_16

❓ Question

I need to move a very large dataset to the cluster. Should I use Globus, and can I transfer it straight into my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/transferring-data-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/transferring-data

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes—**Globus is the recommended tool for large transfers** to FASRC. It's designed for high-throughput, resumable file transfers and is the preferred method for moving large datasets to and from the cluster [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). ### Where to transfer: scratch, not home You should **not** transfer large datasets directly to your home directory. Instead: - **Transfer to scratch or a lab directory** (e.g., `/n/nestscratch` or `/n/holylabs`) for active work. These filesystems are designed for large data and high I/O performance [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). - **Home directories** are intended for small configuration files and personal settings, not large datasets. They have limited space and are not optimized for bulk data transfers. ### Recommended workflow 1. Use Globus to transfer your data to `/n/nestscratch` (scratch) or your group's `/n/holylabs` directory. 2. Once the data is on the cluster, you can move or copy it to other locations as needed. 3. Be aware that scratch has a **90-day retention policy** and is not backed up, so plan accordingly [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/). For detailed Globus setup instructions, see [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, use Globus — it's the recommended alternative to SSH/SCP specifically for very large data sets, and you can install Globus Connect Personal to make your own machine an endpoint. However, you cannot transfer into your home directory: home directories are not available through Globus (and never will be) because sharing them is a security vulnerability. Target a lab or scratch location instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

For detailed Globus setup instructions, see [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/).

Question 18: question_18

❓ Question

I want to run a short GPU job on Cannon. How do I request the GPU and make sure it has enough memory so it isn't killed?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Python/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, slurm.schedmd.com/troubleshoot.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/KHARMA/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise2/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Here's how to request a GPU on Cannon and avoid having your job killed for exceeding memory limits: ### 1. Request the GPU Add the `--gpus` flag to your SLURM script (or interactive `salloc` command) to request the number of GPUs you need: ```bash #SBATCH -p gpu # or gpu_requeue for backfill #SBATCH --gpus=1 # request 1 GPU (change as needed) ``` You can also request CPUs and GPUs independently, e.g.: ```bash #SBATCH -n 4 # 4 CPU cores #SBATCH --gpus=1 # 1 GPU ``` ### 2. Ensure Enough Memory to Avoid OOM Kills Jobs can be killed if they exceed their memory allocation. To prevent this: - **Request sufficient memory** using `--mem` (total per node) or `--mem-per-cpu`: ```bash # Total memory for the whole node #SBATCH --mem=32g # Or memory per CPU core #SBATCH --mem-per-cpu=8g ``` - **Check GPU memory usage** inside your job using `nvidia-smi` to ensure your code isn't consuming more than the GPU's available VRAM. - **Load the CUDA module** in your script so the correct runtime libraries are available: ```bash module load cuda/<version> ``` ### Quick Example (Interactive) ```bash salloc -p gpu -t 0-01:00 --mem=16g --gpus=1 ``` ### Quick Example (Batch Script) ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH -p gpu #SBATCH -t 00:30:00 #SBATCH --gpus=1 #SBATCH --mem=16g #SBATCH -o gpu_job_%j.out module load cuda/12.x srun your_gpu_program ``` For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [GPU Computing on the FASRC cluster](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster).
✅ Expected Answer
Request the GPU with #SBATCH --gpus=1. Don't rely on the default memory — if you omit a memory request you only get 100 MB and the job will likely be killed, so set #SBATCH --mem=<N>G to a value your job needs. Submit to a GPU-capable partition and keep within its time limit (for short tests, use a test/short partition).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

❓ Question

How do I submit a job on TACC Frontera using its queue limits?

🤖 archi's Answer
I don't have specific documentation on TACC Frontera's queue limits in my indexed sources. The results I found are primarily about FASRC clusters and general Slurm configuration, not TACC-specific resources. To submit a job on TACC Frontera respecting its queue limits, you would typically: 1. **Check available partitions and their limits** using: ```bash sinfo -O ``` or ```bash sinfo -p <partition_name> -O ``` 2. **Submit a job with appropriate resource requests** using `sbatch`, specifying resources that fit within the partition's limits: ```bash sbatch --partition=<partition> --nodes=<n> --ntasks-per-node=<n> --time=<HH:MM:SS> --mem=<memory> your_script.sh ``` For accurate, up-to-date information on Frontera's specific queue/partition limits, I recommend checking: - TACC's Frontera documentation at [https://portal.tacc.utexas.edu/tacc-guides/frontera](https://portal.tacc.utexas.edu/tacc-guides/frontera) - Running `sinfo -O` on the cluster to see current partition configurations - TACC's user documentation or contacting TACC support Would you like help with general SLURM job submission syntax instead?
✅ Expected Answer
That's outside FASRC's documentation — Frontera is a TACC system at UT Austin with its own scheduler policies and queues. Check TACC's user guide for Frontera. I can help with the equivalent on FASRC Cannon (SLURM partitions, limits, and submit scripts) if that's useful.

Question 22: question_22

❓ Question

I'm Harvard faculty but I just want to run jobs inside a colleague's existing lab group on the cluster — I don't want my own group and I don't want to deal with billing. Which job title should I pick on the account request form?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/additional-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mcz-staff-compute

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You should select **POSTDOC/RESEARCHER** on the account request form. According to the FASRC documentation, "Harvard faculty who are simply collaborating with another PI should also use this designation to avoid triggering billing and vetting required for a new cluster lab group" [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/). Selecting the "FACULTY" title would trigger the requirements for creating a new lab group and associated billing, which you want to avoid. By using the POSTDOC/RESEARCHER designation, you can join your colleague's existing lab group without incurring those administrative and financial obligations.
✅ Expected Answer
Pick POSTDOC/RESEARCHER. A Harvard faculty member who wants to sign up as an individual collaborator under another faculty member's cluster group should choose POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, because choosing FACULTY requires billing information and starts the new lab setup process (and FACULTY signups require vetting, so they take longer to process).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications

What does someone with non-faculty PI rights have to supply at signup that an ordinary researcher does not?

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, someone with **non-faculty PI rights** must supply **their own 33-digit billing string(s)** at signup, which an ordinary researcher (POSTDOC/RESEARCHER, GRAD STUDENT, etc.) does not need to provide. From the "Tips Before Signup" section of the Account Qualifications page: > "If you have a non-faculty appointment but have PI rights with a supported affiliation and your own 33-digit billing string(s), you should select NON-FACULTY PI (your account and PI rights will need to be vetted before approval. FASRC may contact you for more information)." [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/) Additionally, non-faculty PI accounts require **vetting before approval**, and FASRC may contact the applicant for more information.
✅ Expected Answer
A NON-FACULTY PI must supply a 33-digit billing code at signup. Non-Faculty PI accounts also carry additional controls and privileges, and the signup requires vetting so it takes longer to process.

Question 25: question_25

❓ Question

If a Chan School PI sponsors an outside collaborator's cluster account, who ends up paying for that person's compute time, and what happens to their access if the PI leaves?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health + FAS Research Computing Q&A](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa/), here are the answers to your questions: **Who pays?** There is no up-front cost for the outside collaborator's account, but the collaborator's compute usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and **billed to the Chan School on a quarterly basis**. The collaborator's account must be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account, and you should select "EXTERNAL" in the account request. **What happens if the PI leaves?** If the PI leaves HCSPH, **the collaborator's access would also end** unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing. It is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.
✅ Expected Answer
There is no up-front cost for the collaborator's account, but their cluster usage (in CPU hours) is applied to the sponsoring PI's lab group fairshare and billed to the school on a quarterly basis. The account must be requested as EXTERNAL and be approved by and attached to an existing HCSPH PI's FASRC account. Should the PI leave HCSPH, the collaborator's access also ends unless it is transferred to another PI's account and billing; it is the PI's responsibility to notify FASRC when the collaborator's access should be withdrawn.

Question 27: question_27

❓ Question

Do Harvard Chan people still get charged that yearly per-person fee just to have a Research Computing account?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the annual per-person fee is **no longer charged**. According to the [Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health – FAS Research Computing Overview](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview/): > The previous annual per-user charge for FAS Research Computing accounts is *no longer* used as of the fiscal year 2019. Standard services, including accounts and access to the FAS RC high-performance computing cluster, will be effectively free to all Harvard Chan School community members via the account of an eligible PI. These FASRC computing costs are now covered by the school's overhead rate. So as long as you're affiliated with an eligible PI at Chan, you can get an account and cluster access without a per-person fee. Note that supplemental resources like increased storage or VMs may still involve additional charges — see the school's [Billing FAQ](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/harvard-school-of-public-health/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa) for details.
✅ Expected Answer

Supplemental resources such as increased storage and VMs still involve additional charges.

Question 28: question_28

❓ Question

I'm on FASSE and want to run VS Code on a compute node. Can I just set up Remote-SSH to the compute node the way I do on Cannon?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/vscode_remote_tunnel.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you cannot simply use the same Remote-SSH setup on FASSE as you do on Cannon. The two clusters have different architectures and networking configurations, so the SSH proxy commands and job allocation steps that work on Cannon won't directly translate to FASSE. Here's what you need to know: ## Cannon vs FASSE Differences On Cannon, the typical setup uses a `ProxyCommand` that allocates a SLURM job and uses `nc` (netcat) to tunnel SSH to the compute node. This approach is documented for Cannon specifically [VSCode Remote Development via SSH and Tunnel](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel/). FASSE has a different cluster architecture, so the login nodes, SLURM configuration, and networking will differ. ## Recommended Approach for FASSE Instead of trying to adapt the Cannon SSH setup, consider using the **Remote Tunnel** approach, which is more flexible across different cluster environments: 1. **Login to FASSE** via SSH 2. **Download the VS Code CLI** on the compute node: ```bash curl -Lk 'https://code.visualstudio.com/sha/download?build=stable&os=cli-alpine-x64' --output vscode_cli.tar.gz tar -xzf vscode_cli.tar.gz ``` 3. **Move the `code` executable** to a persistent location (like your LABS folder) 4. **Add it to your PATH** in `~/.bashrc` 5. **Run the tunnel command** on the compute node to establish an interactive connection This tunnel approach works interactively on whatever compute node you're allocated, regardless of the cluster's specific SSH proxy setup. Would you like me to pull the full documentation for the tunnel setup steps, or do you have a specific question about adapting this for FASSE?
✅ Expected Answer
No. You cannot use Remote SSH to connect your local VS Code to a FASSE compute node, because salloc is not permitted on FASSE for security reasons. Remote SSH to FASSE's login node does work. For a FASSE compute node, launching a Remote Tunnel as a batch job (Approach I) is your only option — and before doing that you must enable internet access by setting a network proxy (you are on the FASSE VPN realm, @fasse), including adding the same proxy in your local VS Code settings; remember to unset that proxy in VS Code when you are not on the FASSE VPN, or it will block your other non-FASSE VS Code remote sessions.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 29: question_29

❓ Question

The VS Code Dev Containers extension keeps trying to talk to a Docker daemon on the cluster, which doesn't exist there. What do I have to change so it uses the container runtime FASRC actually provides?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
FASRC provides **Podman** (a daemonless container runtime), not a Docker daemon. To make the VS Code Dev Containers extension work, you need to: ### 1. Start the Podman system service on your compute node In a VS Code terminal on the cluster, run: ```bash nohup podman system service -t 0 >/dev/null 2>&1 & ``` This starts a Podman API socket at `/tmp/containers-user-$(id -u)/podman/podman.sock` on the compute node. ### 2. Update your `devcontainer.json` Add the following settings to bind-mount the Podman socket into the container: ```json { "remoteUser": "root", "mounts": [ "source=/tmp/podman-run-<UID>/podman/podman.sock,target=/var/run/docker-host.sock,type=bind" ], "remoteEnv": { "DOCKER_BUILDKIT": "0" } } ``` Replace `<UID>` with your actual user ID (e.g., `1234`). ### Why this works FASRC's `docker` command is actually a wrapper around **Podman** ([Podman – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman/)), which is daemonless and designed to work with SLURM. The Dev Containers extension expects a Docker-compatible socket, so you start the Podman system service and mount its socket into the dev container as `/var/run/docker-host.sock`. For a complete example, see the [Dev Containers features issue](https://github.com/devcontainers/features/issues/952#issuecomment-3474430006) referenced in the docs.
✅ Expected Answer
Point the Dev Containers extension at Podman. In Code > Settings > Extensions > Dev Containers, change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Path' from 'docker' to 'podman', and change 'Dev > Containers: Docker Socket Path' from '/var/run/docker.sock' to '/tmp/podman-run-<uid>/podman/podman.sock', replacing <uid> with your FASRC user ID (obtained by running 'id -u' on the cluster). If you are using a local VS Code rather than an OOD Remote Desktop, you will need to revert these settings later if you use dev containers with a local Docker installation.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel

Question 30: question_30

❓ Question

I set up TF 2.16 on the cluster with the FASRC conda steps and now Keras blows up saying it has no backend. What environment variable am I supposed to export?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ood-remote-desktop-how-to-open-software, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/home-directory-full, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/AI/TensorFlow/Example2/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You need to export: ```bash export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" ``` Starting with TensorFlow 2.16.1, Keras 3.0 is included, and it requires this environment variable to be set. You can add it to your conda environment's activation script so it's set automatically each time you activate the environment: ```bash mkdir -p $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d echo 'export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"' >> $CONDA_PREFIX/etc/conda/activate.d/env_vars.sh ``` See the [TensorFlow installation guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow/) for full details.
✅ Expected Answer
Export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow". Starting with version 2.16.1, TensorFlow includes Keras 3.0, so the Keras backend must be set to TF. FASRC's instructions include `export KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow"` in both the GPU and CPU conda recipes, and prefix the singularity runs with it as well, e.g. `KERAS_BACKEND="tensorflow" singularity exec --nv tf2.16.1_gpu.simg python3`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tensorflow

Question 32: question_32

❓ Question

My PyTorch job on gpu_test sees a device but the card looks sliced up and I only get a fraction of it. What do I need to do differently on that partition?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/mmpose.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/pytorch/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On the `gpu_test` partition, GPUs are configured in **MIG (Multi-Instance GPU) mode**, which slices the card into smaller instances. PyTorch needs to know which MIG instance to use. You need to explicitly set the `CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES` environment variable to the MIG instance ID. You can do this interactively: 1. Get the GPU card name (the MIG instance ID): ```bash nvidia-smi -L ``` 2. Set the environment variable to the MIG instance you want: ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-<your-instance-id> ``` Or, you can automate this in your job script: ```bash export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}') ``` This step is **not** necessary on the standard `gpu` partition, only on `gpu_test` where MIG is active [PyTorch – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pytorch/).
✅ Expected Answer
gpu_test is currently the only FASRC partition with MIG (multi-instance GPU) mode enabled, so you must target a MIG instance explicitly. Get the GPU/MIG instance name with `nvidia-smi -L`, then set CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES to the MIG instance, e.g. `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=MIG-5b36b802-0ab0-5f37-af2d-ac23f40ef62d`, or automate it with `export CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=$(nvidia-smi -L | awk '/MIG/ {gsub(/[()]/,"");print $NF}')`.

Question 33: question_33

❓ Question

Our CryoSPARC master keeps coming up bound to the wrong machine name so the workers can't reach it. Where is that hostname actually set?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/bashrc_additions.sh, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The hostname is set in the CryoSPARC Master configuration file: **`cryosparc_master/config.sh`** Specifically, look for the `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` variable. A typical entry looks like: ```bash export CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME=holy_______.rc.fas.harvard.edu ``` If you leave `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` unset, CryoSPARC will default to whatever hostname the machine reports when you run `cryosparcm start` — which is likely why it's binding to the wrong name when you move between nodes or sessions. ### Quick fix 1. Edit your config file: ```bash $CRYOSPARC_INSTALL_DIR/cryosparc_master/config.sh ``` 2. Set `CRYOSPARC_MASTER_HOSTNAME` to the **actual** hostname of the node where the master will run. 3. Restart the master: ```bash cryosparcm restart ``` If you also changed the base port, run `cryosparcm fixdbport` followed by `cryosparcm restart` to update the database. See the FASRC docs for more details: [CryoSPARC – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cryosparc/)
✅ Expected Answer
It is set in the CryoSPARC master config file, `cryosparc_master/config.sh`, which holds the license, the path to the MongoDB database, the master hostname, and the base TCP port. The hostname of the node running the master application is typically hardcoded in that config.sh; if it is left unset, CryoSPARC takes the hostname of the machine on which `cryosparcm start` is called. If these config.sh settings are not correct you will experience errors. Note also that the TCP ports of the individual component programs are not individually configurable — only the base port the user connects to is.

Question 35: question_35

❓ Question

Google finally approved my AlphaFold 3 request and sent me the weights as a .bin.zst download. Where am I supposed to unpack that on Cannon — can I just leave it in netscratch?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/alphafold_v3.def, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/run_alphafold_multi.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/alphapose.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you should **not** leave the unpacked weights in `netscratch`. According to the FASRC AlphaFold documentation: > Google will provide a file `file_name.bin.zst`. Extract with `unzstd file.bin.zst`. Place `file.bin` in a **lab share** (do not put in netscratch) — this will be the location of your `--model_dir`. So the steps are: 1. **Unpack** the file: ```bash unzstd file_name.bin.zst ``` 2. **Move** the resulting `file_name.bin` into your **lab share** (e.g., `/n/holylustre/...` or wherever your group's shared storage is mounted). This is required because the `--model_dir` flag in your AlphaFold 3 run needs to point to a location that's accessible to the job, and lab shares are the supported location for model parameters.
✅ Expected Answer
Extract the file Google sends you with `unzstd file.bin.zst`, then place the resulting `file.bin` in a lab share — do NOT put it in netscratch. That location is what you pass as `--model_dir` when you run AlphaFold3.

Question 36: question_36

❓ Question

Before I can run an AlphaFold3 prediction on the cluster, do I need to download the AlphaFold databases into my own lab storage first? If they're already there, what's the path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No, you do not need to download the AlphaFold databases yourself.** FASRC maintains them in a cluster-wide location on SSD storage. ### AlphaFold 3 database path ``` /n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3 ``` ### AlphaFold 2 database path (for reference) ``` /n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2 ``` ### What you *do* need to provide For AlphaFold 3, you must obtain the model parameters from Google and place them in your lab storage (not netscratch). The extracted `file.bin` goes to a directory like: ``` /n/holylabs/LABS/<your_lab>/Lab/alphafold3/model_parameters ``` This directory is passed to the `--model_dir` flag when running. ### Example usage The example scripts bind the cluster database to `/data` inside the Singularity container: ```bash singularity exec \ --bind /n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3:/data \ /n/singularity_images/FAS/alphafold/alphafold_3.0.1.sif \ python /app/alphafold/run_alphafold.py \ --db_dir=/data \ --model_dir=/n/holylabs/LABS/<your_lab>/Lab/alphafold3/model_parameters \ ... ``` See the full example scripts and setup guide at [FASRC User_Codes AlphaFold README](https://github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/AlphaFold/README.md) and the [AlphaFold documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/alphafold/).
✅ Expected Answer
No download is needed. The AlphaFold database is kept in a cluster-wide location (on SSD, as the developers recommend), so individual users do not have to download it. The AlphaFold3 database is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v3` (AlphaFold2's is at `/n/holylabs/rc_admin/Everyone/alphafold_databases/v2`). Likewise, the Singularity images are cluster-wide at `/n/singularity_images/FAS/alphafold/`, so you do not have to copy those either.

Question 37: question_37

❓ Question

I copied a friend's sbatch script and stripped it down — it has no partition line, no memory line, and no time line. What does the scheduler actually hand me?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/R/Example1/run.sbatch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/multicore.slurm, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Applications/CryoSPARC/configure.sh, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/basicbatch.slurm, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_submit_plugins.html, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you omit `#SBATCH --partition`, `#SBATCH --mem`, and `#SBATCH --time` from an sbatch script, SLURM uses the cluster’s default values for those parameters. Here’s what typically happens: - **Partition**: SLURM assigns the job to the **default partition** for your account. On most FASRC clusters, this is usually `standard` or `workq`, but the exact default depends on the cluster and your account’s allocation. You can check your default partition by running `sinfo -p` or `sacctmgr show qos` to see which partitions your account has access to. - **Memory**: If you don’t specify `--mem`, SLURM uses the **default memory limit** for the partition, which is often **1 GB per node** (or sometimes per core, depending on cluster configuration). If your job needs more, it will be killed with an `Out of Memory` error. Always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu` explicitly to avoid this. - **Time**: If you don’t specify `--time`, SLURM uses the **default time limit** for the partition, which is typically **24 hours** on many FASRC clusters. If your job runs longer, it will be killed. ### Recommended Action To avoid surprises, always explicitly set these in your sbatch script: ```bash #SBATCH --partition=standard #SBATCH --mem=4G # or --mem-per-cpu=2G #SBATCH --time=02:00:00 # 2 hours ``` ### How to Check Defaults You can inspect the current defaults by running: ```bash sinfo -o "%P %m %t" # Shows partition, memory, and time limits sacctmgr show qos # Shows QoS (Quality of Service) defaults ``` If you’re unsure what your account’s defaults are, run `sbatch --test-only your_script.sh` — this will print out the job configuration SLURM would use **without actually submitting it**. Would you like help checking the defaults for your specific cluster or account?
✅ Expected Answer
FASRC's defaults when nothing is requested are: 1 core, 100 MB of memory, 0 GPUs, and the `serial_requeue` partition. Time is the exception — there is no default time limit, so you must always declare a time yourself. The 100 MB memory default is very likely to get your job killed, so you should always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu`.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs
💬 Agent Messages (1)
🤖 Assistant Message #1

Here’s what typically happens: - **Partition**: SLURM assigns the job to the **default partition** for your account. On most FASRC clusters, this is usually `standard` or `workq`, but the exact default depends on the cluster and your account’s allocation. You can check your default partition by running `sinfo -p` or `sacctmgr show qos` to see which partitions your account has access to. - **Memory**: If you don’t specify `--mem`, SLURM uses the **default memory limit** for the partition, which is often **1 GB per node** (or sometimes per core, depending on cluster configuration). If your job needs more, it will be killed with an `Out of Memory` error. Always specify `--mem` or `--mem-per-cpu` explicitly to avoid this. - **Time**: If you don’t specify `--time`, SLURM uses the **default time limit** for the partition, which is typically **24 hours** on many FASRC clusters. Retrieved Documents: slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurm.conf.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, slurm.schedmd.com/cons_tres_share.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/FFTW/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Parallel_HDF5/Example2/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/IQSS-slurm-examples/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Example_Recipes/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — you can submit them as a single job array, but **12,000 tasks exceeds FASRC's maximum array size**. ### FASRC limits - **Maximum Array Size:** 10,000 (both the number of array indices and the size of the array) [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/) - **Maximum Number of Jobs per User:** 10,100 (each array index counts as one job toward this limit) [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/) ### What this means for you 1. **You cannot fit 12,000 tasks into a single `--array` job.** The hard cap is 10,000. 2. **Workarounds:** - **Split into two arrays:** Submit one array of 10,000 and a second of 2,000. Each array counts as one job toward your 10,100 limit, so you'd use 2 of your 10,100 jobs. - **Chunking:** Submit multiple smaller arrays (e.g., six arrays of 2,000 each). This also keeps you within limits and gives you finer control over submission and monitoring. ### Does it eat into your queued-job count? - **Yes, but efficiently.** Each array *task* counts as one job toward your 10,100 per-user limit. So 12,000 tasks would exceed the limit regardless of whether they're in one or multiple arrays. - However, each *array itself* counts as **one job** for purposes of commands like `squeue -u $USER` and for holding/resuming. So managing one or two arrays is much cleaner than submitting 12,000 individual jobs. ### Example submission (10,000-task array) ```bash sbatch --array=0-9999%100 myscript.sh ``` The `%100` limits concurrent array tasks to 100 at a time, which helps avoid overwhelming the scheduler or storage. For more detail, see [Running Jobs: Job Arrays](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/#Job_arrays) and the [SLURM job array documentation](https://slurm.schedmd.com/job_array.html).
✅ Expected Answer
No — you must split it. The maximum array size is 10,000 (that cap applies to both the array index and the array size), so a single 12,000-element array is not allowed. And yes, it counts against your job cap: each array index counts as a single job for the purposes of the Maximum Number of Jobs per User, which is 10,100.

Question 39: question_39

❓ Question

My analysis takes about 3 hours every time on the shared partition, but when I run the exact same thing on serial_requeue it keeps getting killed for hitting the wall clock. What time should I be asking for there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-research-computing-overview, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your job takes about 3 hours on the shared partition, you should request **9 hours** on the `serial_requeue` partition. The `serial_requeue` partition can have substantially slower hardware, and a good rule of thumb is to allow for a factor of three variance in speed. So if your job takes 3 hours on most hardware, give it 9 hours on `serial_requeue` as you may end up on a substantially slower host [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/).
✅ Expected Answer
Ask for roughly 9 hours. `serial_requeue` (and `gpu_requeue`) are mosaic partitions made of a wide variety of hardware, so run times vary a lot depending on which host you land on. The rule of thumb is a factor of three variance in speed — so a job that takes 3 hours on most hardware should be given 9 hours on `serial_requeue`. Alternatively, use the `--constraint` option to pin the job to a specific hardware type so the runtime is predictable.
🎯 Expected Source Documents

Question 40: question_40

❓ Question

My PI bought the nodes in our lab's own partition, and we only ever run there. So why does our fairshare keep dropping — aren't we just using hardware we already paid for?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/geophysics-partitions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/huce-partitions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
It’s a common misconception, but **Fairshare is a global metric**, not tied to specific hardware. Here’s why your Fairshare drops even if you only use your lab’s purchased nodes: * **Fairshare is global:** Fairshare is calculated based on your group’s total usage across the entire cluster, regardless of which partition you run on [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). * **You are still "charged":** Even if you run exclusively on a private partition, your group is granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, but your usage is still tracked globally. Because Fairshare is a global quantity, you are charged for your runs regardless of what partition you use [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). * **Hardware doesn't immediately fix it:** While purchasing hardware does eventually increase your RawShares (which helps improve your Fairshare over time), it takes time for the hardware to be built, installed, and for the system to recalculate your share [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/). In short, using "your" nodes doesn't exempt you from the global Fairshare accounting system. If you want to improve your Fairshare, you may need to look into adjusting your group's RawShares or contact FASRC directly for more information on purchasing hardware [Fairshare and Job Accounting – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare/).
✅ Expected Answer
Because Fairshare is a global quantity on Cannon, not a per-partition one: RawShares are granted to everyone and can be used anywhere on the cluster, so a user is charged regardless of which partition they run in. Groups that own private partitions are granted RawShares equivalent to the hardware in that partition, and that grant exactly offsets the usage of the partition — so it nets out. The upside of this design is flexibility: because the Share is global, a group can leave its own partition idle and spend its shares elsewhere on the cluster instead.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 41: question_41

❓ Question

Last week a bunch of our jobs died from node failures and we cancelled several more that were misconfigured. Does that wasted compute still count against our lab's score, and how long until it stops hurting us?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, slurm.schedmd.com/job_state_codes.html, slurm.schedmd.com/resource_limits.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sinfo.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sacct.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/common-pitfalls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/convenient-slurm-commands, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/seas-compute-resources, slurm.schedmd.com/quickstart.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/lab-groups, slurm.schedmd.com/qos.html

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available FASRC documentation, here's what I can tell you: ### Does wasted compute count against your score? **Yes, it does.** FASRC's FairShare system charges your lab's score for the resources you *request and are allocated*, not just what you actually use. From the [Job Efficiency and Optimization Best Practices](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-efficiency-and-optimization-best-practices/) guide: - **Time**: Slurm does *not* charge you for time you don't use — if you ask for 3 days but only use 2 hours, you're charged for 2 hours. - **Memory, Cores, and GPUs**: You *are* charged for these whether you use them or not, because the scheduler blocks them off for you and can't give them to others. So if your jobs were allocated resources and then died (whether from node failure or cancellation), the allocated cores, memory, and GPUs for the time they ran would count against your FairShare score. ### How long until it stops hurting you? The documentation mentions that FairShare is a **temporary** score that recovers over time — "the more jobs you run, the lower your score becomes, temporarily" ([FAQ](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/)). However, the specific decay rate or recovery timeline isn't detailed in the indexed documentation. To check your current score and see how it's trending, you can run: ```bash sshare -u RCUSERNAME ``` Your effective score appears in the last column, where a score ≤ 0.5 indicates lower priority and > 0.5 indicates higher priority ([FAQ](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq/)). ### For node-failure jobs specifically Jobs that die from `NODE_FAILURE` are automatically requeued by the scheduler ([Running Jobs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/)). If your jobs were requeued and ran again, you'd be charged again for the second run. If they were cancelled by you before running, no compute was consumed. ### Recommendation For details on the exact decay timeline and how to monitor your score recovery, I'd recommend contacting [RCHelp](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/) directly, as the specific FairShare decay parameters may be internal cluster configuration details not fully documented in the public-facing guides.
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, it still counts. RawUsage is the sum of all usage including failed jobs, jobs that are requeued, and jobs that ran on nodes that failed — FASRC counts it because it is the user's responsibility to use the allocated time effectively even when it is cut short. That usage does decay, though: RawUsage is attenuated by the cluster's half-life, currently 3 days. Work done in the last 3 days counts at full cost, work done 6 days ago costs half, work done 9 days ago one fourth, and so on. To limit the damage from failures, FASRC recommends testing/verifying code before running and enabling checkpointing so jobs can restart where they left off.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

Question 42: question_42

❓ Question

I'm a master's student and my advisor wants me to run analyses on the Nielsen consumer panel data. Can I be added to the agreement?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the [Nielsen Dataset documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset/), **no, you cannot be added to the agreement as a master's student.** The agreement explicitly states that data access is limited to: * PhD students * Postdocs * Tenure-track faculty It specifically notes that **"Data is not available to undergraduates or masters students."** Access is granted solely to individual researchers (PhD students, postdocs, or faculty) who are registered on the Chicago Booth Nielsen site.
✅ Expected Answer
No. Under the Master Access Agreement, the Nielsen data and license are made solely to the individual researcher registered via the Chicago Booth Nielsen site. That researcher can be a Ph.D. student or a postdoc who has a tenure-track faculty member registered on the site — but the data is explicitly not available to undergraduates or master's students. Relatedly, under the Data Access and Confidentiality Agreement the Researcher may disclose the Data only to PhD-seeking students of Harvard working under their supervision on the Research Project.

Question 44: question_44

❓ Question

I want to spin up a few hundred cores on Cannon to crawl a public website faster. It's for academic research — any issue with that?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/GPU_Computing/Dist_GPU_Computing/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-figure-out-how-efficient-my-job-is, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/training-materials, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acceptable-use, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openai-guidelines

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes — there are significant issues with running a large-scale web crawl from Cannon. **FASRC's Web Scraping Policy** explicitly states: - **"We *highly* discourage using the cluster itself to scrape data."** Because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is "easily weaponized" and your actions could have consequences for other researchers. - **"You should contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster."** - It is *highly preferable* to do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster. The policy also notes that: - Fair use generally applies to human information gathering, **not** programmatic machine scraping. - As a Harvard representative, your scraping uses the target site's servers and bandwidth in ways the site may not approve, which can lead to **IP blacklisting and even legal action**. - Academic purpose does **not** exempt you from the target site's terms of service. **What you should do instead:** 1. **Scrape elsewhere** — use a personal machine, a cloud instance, or a service designed for web crawling. 2. **Contact FASRC** before attempting any scraping on the cluster. 3. If the data is sensitive or contains human subjects, get it reviewed for compliance before placing it on the cluster. For more guidance, the policy recommends contacting the Harvard [Office of the General Counsel](https://ogc.harvard.edu/) or [Office of the Vice Provost for Research](https://vpr.harvard.edu/). See the full policy: [Web Scraping Policy](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/web-scraping-policy/)
✅ Expected Answer
Yes, several. FASRC highly discourages using the cluster itself to scrape: because of its size and ease of parallelization, the cluster is easily weaponized and your actions could have consequences for other researchers, so you should seek another avenue for data acquisition first. You must contact FASRC before commencing any scraping activity using the FASRC cluster. It is highly preferable that you do the scraping elsewhere and then bring the data to the cluster for processing — and if the data is sensitive, confidential, contains human data, or it is unclear, doing it elsewhere is a requirement. Highly parallelized scraping is specifically called out as risking blacklisting of the cluster's or the university's IP range, which would harm other users. Being an academic does not exempt you from a site's usage policies.

Question 45: question_45

❓ Question

I logged in to Globus and picked the Harvard FAS RC Holyoke collection, but I can't browse into my home directory and the Everyone folder in my lab share doesn't show up either. Is my account misconfigured?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/offboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, this is actually expected behavior and **not a sign that your account is misconfigured**. Here is what is happening: 1. **Home Directory:** Globus typically provides access to specific data directories (like your Lab share) rather than your full home directory (`/home/username`). If you cannot browse into your home directory via Globus, it is likely because Globus is not configured to expose that path by default. You should use SSH/SCP or the OnDemand file manager to access your home directory. 2. **"Everyone" Folder:** The "Everyone" folder in your lab share is **not available on Globus**. According to the [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/) documentation, the "Everyone" directory is intended for internal sharing on the HPC cluster and is explicitly excluded from Globus access. **What you should do:** * Use **Globus** to access your **Lab** directory (the primary working directory shared via Globus). * Use **SSH/SCP** or the **Open OnDemand file manager** to access your **Home** directory or the **Everyone** folder. Your account is likely configured correctly; you are just trying to access paths that Globus does not expose.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is misconfigured — both are expected. Home directories are not and will not be added to Globus under any circumstances, because sharing home directories creates a security vulnerability (authentication keys/passwords could be shared inadvertently). And within a lab share, Globus can only see the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` folders — anything outside those, such as `Everyone` and `Transfer`, is not available through Globus (note the `Users` folder has been deprecated as of 2025). Also be aware symlinks do not work in Globus. If your lab share genuinely lacks a `Lab` directory or is not visible at all in the Holyoke/Boston collections, contact FASRC.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Question 46: question_46

❓ Question

I need to move a directory holding roughly half a million small output files off the cluster with Globus. What's the recommended way to package that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
For a directory with ~500,000 small files, the recommended approach is to **create one or more `tar` archives** before transferring via Globus. Transferring hundreds of thousands of individual files through Globus is extremely slow and error-prone. ### Recommended steps 1. **Plan your archive size** — FASRC recommends keeping individual tar files reasonably sized (e.g., 50–200 GB) to reduce the risk of total data loss from corruption [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/). If your data is large, split it into multiple archives, ideally aligned with directory boundaries. 2. **Create a file list and checksum** (optional but recommended for integrity): ```bash cd /path/to/your/data find mydir/ -type f > file-list.txt find mydir/ -type f -print0 | xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum | sort > checksums.shasum ``` 3. **Create the tar archive(s)** — for example: ```bash tar -cf mydir.tar mydir/ ``` If the data is large, split it: ```bash tar -cf - mydir/ | split -b 100G - mydir.tar.part ``` 4. **Transfer the tar file(s) via Globus** — Globus handles large files much more reliably than thousands of small ones. You can use the Globus web UI, CLI, or API to move the archive to your destination. 5. **Verify integrity** on the receiving end using the checksum file: ```bash shasum -c checksums.shasum ``` ### Why this matters - Transferring 500,000 individual files through Globus will be very slow and may fail partway through. - A single (or a few) tar archive(s) dramatically reduces the number of transfer operations and improves reliability. - Checksums let you verify that nothing was corrupted during transfer. For more detail, see the FASRC guide on [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/).
✅ Expected Answer
Tar the directory into subset files first. FASRC recommends that your tar files range in size from 1-100 GiB — that size range transfers more quickly (especially if the transfer is interrupted) and is smaller to retrieve if it ever needs to come back from Tape. If you leave hundreds or thousands of files in a single directory, Globus will go into an 'endpoint is too busy' state and your job will timeout, restart, timeout, etc. FASRC also recommends reading their 'Tips for using tar to archive data' page before tarring.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer
