Question 47: question_47

❓ Question

I ran shasum on a folder before I tarred it up, and when I extracted the archive later and hashed it again the values don't match, even though every file looks fine. What am I doing wrong?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most common reason your checksums don't match after extracting a tar archive is that **the directory structure or file paths changed** between when you generated the checksum and when you verified it. Here are the key things to check: 1. **Working directory / path differences** — If you ran `shasum` from one directory (e.g., `/n/mypath/scans`) but extracted the tar into a different location or with different relative paths, the filenames in the checksum file won't match the actual files. 2. **Hidden files or symlinks** — If your original `shasum` command didn't include hidden files (those starting with `.`) or didn't follow symlinks consistently, the extracted archive may contain different files than what was hashed. 3. **File metadata differences** — While `shasum` only hashes file *contents*, if the extraction process changed file permissions, ownership, or timestamps in a way that affected subsequent operations, it could cause confusion. However, this wouldn't directly change the hash values. 4. **Active filesystem** — If the data was modified between when you ran the initial checksum and when you created the tar, the checksum will no longer match later when you un-tar and compare. **Best practice from FASRC documentation:** - Generate your checksum file from the same working directory where you'll later verify it - Use a consistent command like: `find myscans/ -type f -print0|xargs --null -P $(nproc) shasum|sort > checksums.shasum` - Verify you're comparing against the same files by checking the file list For more details on proper archiving and checksum procedures, see [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving).
✅ Expected Answer
The checksum depends on the path you hand to find, so the same files hashed two different ways produce two different values. FASRC's guidance is to always do it the exact same way: cd to the directory *above* the one you are archiving (e.g. /n/mypath/scans) and then run find against the relative directory name (myscans) — not the full path. Two other things will break the comparison: (1) modifying the data after you took the checksum but before you tarred it (a checksum is useless on an active filesystem), and (2) storing the checksum file itself inside the tar, since its presence changes the checksum unless you remove it after un-tarring and before re-running.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Question 48: question_48

❓ Question

RC made a tar of my project with Starfish. I copied it to netscratch and extracted it there, but instead of my ProjectX folder I got this weird nested tree of empty directories with ProjectX way down at the bottom. Is the archive corrupt?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, the archive is not corrupt. This is expected behavior when using Starfish-generated tar archives. When Starfish creates a tar archive, it captures the **entire absolute path** of the files inside the archive. So when you extract it, it recreates that full path relative to your current directory. For example, if you tarred `/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX` using Starfish, and then extracted it in `/n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/`, you'll find your data at: ``` /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX ``` This is why you see a nested tree of empty directories leading down to `ProjectX` — those are the intermediate path components (`n`, `holylabs`, `jharvard_lab`) being recreated. **To avoid this**, you can extract the archive into a specific directory using the `-C` flag: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar -C /desired/output/directory ``` Or, if you want to strip the leading path components, you can use `--strip-components`: ```bash tar -xf mystarfishtar.tar --strip-components=3 -C /desired/output/directory ``` (Adjust the number based on how many path components you want to remove.) See [Tips for using tar to archive data](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving/) for more details.
✅ Expected Answer
The archive is fine. Starfish tars in place and captures the entire path inside the tar file, so extracting recreates that whole path underneath your output directory. Extracting a Starfish tar of /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX into /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp gives you /n/netscratch/jharvard/temp/n/holylabs/jharvard_lab/ProjectX. Move the ProjectX directory to where you actually want it, then remove the leftover empty n/holylabs/jharvard_lab tree. Use plain `rm` rather than `rm -rf`, so it errors out if other files remain or you typed the wrong path.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving

Question 49: question_49

❓ Question

I already pushed a bunch of archives into our lab's tape area on Globus and then realized I named them badly. Can I just rename them, or move a couple into a different folder to tidy things up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tips-for-tar-archiving, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Unfortunately, the documentation doesn't explicitly address renaming or moving files that are already staged on tape via Globus. However, here's what I can tell you based on the docs: **What you can do:** - The tape staging area is disk-based and available via Globus, so you should be able to use the Globus File Manager to rename or move files within your lab's tape collection. - However, **permissions are set at the collection level** — there's no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection, and read/write access applies to the whole collection [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). **What to watch out for:** - Once data is **migrated to tape** (not just sitting in the staging area), it may not be directly accessible for renaming/moving via Globus. The staging area is where you write data before it gets migrated to tape [Globus: Transfer Data to Tape](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access/). - If your files are already on tape (not just in staging), you may need to retrieve them to staging first, or contact support. **Recommendation:** Try renaming or moving the files through the Globus File Manager first. If the files are already migrated to tape and you can't access them, reach out to FASRC support at rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu for guidance on retrieving and reorganizing tape-stored data.
✅ Expected Answer
No — do not delete, move, or rename data once it has been transferred to the tape buffer. The Globus buffer gives you write access to upload data destined for tape, and all changes within your lab's Globus collection are automatically picked up by the tape robot. Deletions on the Globus buffer may result in permanent deletion on tape, and deleting files that have already been placed on tape removes the 'link' and makes retrieval impossible. Mass deletions, moves, and file renaming require cumbersome manual intervention. Name files appropriately before you transfer them, and work with your PI or lab data manager on your lab's tape storage protocols.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Args: No query found.

Question 50: question_50

❓ Question

Our PI wants to let a postdoc read and write only one project folder inside our lab's tape collection on Globus, not the rest of it. How do we set that up?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-modernization-initiative-faqs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Globus on FASRC exposes only the `Lab` and `Users/$USER` directories, and it inherits your POSIX permissions — meaning Globus can see everything your FASRC account can access inside those top-level folders [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). Because of this, you **cannot** restrict a Globus share to a single subfolder at the Globus level. Instead, you need to control access through POSIX permissions on the filesystem itself. Here’s how: ### Recommended approach: Use POSIX permissions + a dedicated group 1. **Create a dedicated group** (e.g., `lab_postdoc_access`) and add only the PI and the postdoc to it. 2. **Change the group ownership** of the specific project folder to that group: ```bash chgrp -R lab_postdoc_access /n/lab_name/Lab/project_folder ``` 3. **Set permissions** so that only the owner and group members can read/write: ```bash chmod -R 770 /n/lab_name/Lab/project_folder ``` 4. **Remove group/world access** from sibling folders in `Lab/` that the postdoc should **not** see: ```bash chmod -R 700 /n/lab_name/Lab/other_project_folder ``` This way, the postdoc’s FASRC account (and thus their Globus session) can only access the project folder they have POSIX permissions for. ### Important caveats - **Symlinks do not work in Globus**, so you can’t work around this by symlinking a subfolder [Globus File Transfer](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). - FASRC **highly recommends** keeping lab storage open to all lab members for collaboration and cleanup, so consider whether restricting access aligns with your lab’s norms [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm). If you need more granular control, you may want to discuss options with FASRC support, as Globus itself doesn’t support folder-level scoping.
✅ Expected Answer
You cannot. There is no way to restrict permissions on individual folders within your lab tape collection — read/write access applies to the whole lab collection on Globus. The underlying filesystem is not owned by FASRC, so FASRC cannot set those permissions; everyone with access is essentially a guest on the NESE tape system. (The PI and General Manager can add lab members to the collection, but only to all of it.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid

Question 51: question_51

❓ Question

On Cannon I just do salloc to grab a shell on a compute node, but on FASSE it doesn't work. How am I supposed to get an interactive session there?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/matlab, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/knime-on-the-fasrc-clusters, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/about-usernames, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/gpgpu-computing-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/modules-intro, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the documentation, here's what's happening and what you should do: ## Why `salloc` doesn't work the same way on FASSE FASSE is a **secure environment** cluster ([IQSS FASSE Quickstart Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-fasse-quickstart-guide/)) with different access patterns than Cannon. The key differences are: ### On FASSE, you primarily use **Open OnDemand (OOD)** for interactive sessions Instead of using `salloc` directly from the command line, FASSE is designed around the web-based interface at **[https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu](https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu)**. ### How to get an interactive session on FASSE: **Option 1: Predefined applications (quick launch)** 1. Log into [FASSE OOD](https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu) 2. Click on the icon of the application you want to use from the quick launch tiles 3. Wait for the job to start (may take a few minutes) 4. Click "Connect to…" or "Launch…" to open the application **Option 2: Custom interactive session** 1. Log into [FASSE OOD](https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu) 2. Choose an application from the **Interactive Apps** menu at the top 3. Fill in custom CPU, RAM, and runtime details 4. Click Launch 5. Navigate to the **My Interactive Sessions** page to monitor progress 6. Click "Connect to…" or "Launch…" when ready ### If you need command-line access The login nodes on FASSE have strict resource limits (1 core, 4GB RAM via cgroups) and are meant only for light tasks like preparing jobs or monitoring ([Rocky 8 Transition Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide/)). For anything more involved, you should use the OOD interface or submit jobs through Slurm rather than trying to run interactively on login nodes. Would you like more details on setting up a specific type of interactive session on FASSE?
✅ Expected Answer
salloc is deliberately unavailable on FASSE — it is disabled for security reasons, and FASSE does not allow interactive jobs from the command line at all. For interactive work you use the FASSE Open OnDemand (OOD) / VDI virtual desktop at https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, reachable in your browser while connected to the FASRC VPN on the @fasse realm (note @fasse, not the @fasrc realm you use for Cannon). OOD gives you pre-built apps and interactive command-line access in a desktop-like environment. SSH to fasselogin.rc.fas.harvard.edu remains available for batch work.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse

How long can a job run on the FASSE H200 GPU nodes, and how many GPUs does each of those nodes have?

To request GPUs, include `#SBATCH --gres=gpu:n` in your SLURM submission script, where `n` is 1–4 [Running Jobs – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs/).

Question 53: question_53

❓ Question

A data provider will only let me download their dataset if I give them an IP address to add to their allowlist. What address do they see when I pull the data from a FASSE compute node?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/pi-responsibilities-at-fas-rc

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you download data from a FASSE compute node, the external server will see the IP address of the **FASRC proxy server** (`rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu`), since all outbound internet traffic from FASSE must go through this proxy. Specifically: - **Proxy host:** `rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu` - **Proxy port:** `3128` This means the data provider will see the proxy's IP address (or possibly a pool of IPs) rather than the individual compute node's IP. You should check with the data provider whether they can allow the proxy IP range, or if they require a specific static IP. If the proxy uses NAT with a limited set of outgoing IPs, you may need to ask them to allow those specific addresses. For more details on FASSE proxy configuration, see [FASSE Proxy Settings](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings).
✅ Expected Answer
140.247.232.228. All traffic to the Internet from FASSE — VPN, login, compute, or fasseood — goes out through the FASRC proxy (rcproxy.rc.fas.harvard.edu), which currently has that external IP address. It is subject to change, so you can confirm it by running `curl icanhazip.com` from the node you will actually be working on.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/proxy-settings

Question 54: question_54

❓ Question

Our data use agreement says the data has to be encrypted at rest. I was planning to stage the intermediate files for my FASSE jobs in netscratch since it's fast. Is that OK?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/nielsen-dataset, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/hsph-fas-research-computing-qa, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-security-information-on-storage-and-use-of-confidential-data-hrci, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, slurm.schedmd.com/rest.html, slurm.schedmd.com/jwt.html, slurm.schedmd.com/slurmdbd.conf.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/HDF5/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, **there is no explicit statement that `/n/netscratch` provides encryption at rest**, and scratch filesystems like netscratch are generally designed for high-performance, temporary storage rather than long-term, compliant data retention. Here are the key points from the documentation: 1. **You are responsible for compliance:** The documentation emphasizes that you are responsible for complying with any applicable Data Use Agreements (DUA), Harvard Information Security Policy, and Harvard Research Data Security Policy [FASSE / Protected Data Transfers](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers/). The project PI is also responsible for ensuring compliance [Data and Data Use Agreements (DUA)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-use-agreements/). 2. **Scratch is for temporary data:** Netscratch is a high-performance scratch filesystem (part of the VAST infrastructure) intended for fast, temporary intermediate data during job execution. It is not typically classified as a secure, long-term storage tier for sensitive or restricted data. 3. **Encryption at rest is not guaranteed for scratch:** The documentation does not confirm that netscratch volumes are encrypted at rest. Scratch filesystems are often optimized for I/O performance, which can sometimes come at the cost of additional overhead like encryption. **Recommendation:** * **Do not assume netscratch is compliant** with your DUA's encryption-at-rest requirement. * **Consult your PI and FASRC Support** to confirm whether your specific DUA allows staging sensitive data on netscratch. They may require you to use a different storage tier (e.g., a dedicated, encrypted volume or a compliant data repository) for any data covered by the agreement, even for intermediate files. * **If encryption at rest is mandatory,** you may need to encrypt the data yourself before writing it to netscratch, or use a storage service that explicitly guarantees encryption at rest for sensitive data. Please reach out to FASRC Support or your PI to get explicit confirmation for your specific data classification and DUA requirements.
✅ Expected Answer
No. If you have a DUA that requires encryption at rest, you must not use scratch for any data the DUA applies to — neither local scratch nor FASRC's global scratch supports encryption at rest. Separately, DSL 3 / FASSE data should not be stored in your home directory either. You are responsible for knowing and complying with the applicable Harvard Information Security Policy, the Harvard Research Data Security Policy, and any applicable contracts / data use agreements; if you are unsure how this applies to your data, consult FASRC at security@rc.fas.harvard.edu or your School Security Officer.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Question 55: question_55

❓ Question

Our level 3 project storage doesn't show up as a collection in Globus at all. What do we have to do to get it added?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fasse-protected-data-transfers, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Globus/transfer_globus.py, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation, **data security level 3 storage shares are not automatically added to Globus** [Globus File Transfer – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer/). To get your level 3 project storage added to Globus, you need to **contact FASRC** directly. The documentation states: > "If you find that your lab share is not seen by Globus or these sub directories don't exist in your lab's share, in the Holyoke or Boston collections, please contact us." Specifically, for level 3 data, you would use one of the **Secure** collections: - **"Harvard FAS RC Holyoke Secure"** — for level 3 lab shares in the Holyoke data center - **"Harvard FAS RC Boston Secure"** — for level 3 lab shares in the Boston data center Once your storage is added, note that Globus can only see folders inside `Lab` and `Users/$USER` — directories like `Everyone` and `Transfer` are not available through Globus.
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is broken: DSL 3 / FASSE storage is intentionally not included in Globus by default. To have it exposed, first review the governing DUA — Globus makes data available over the Internet without a VPN or private network, so a DUA requiring VPN/private-network access for all access to the data would not be Globus-compliant (consult your School Security Officer if unsure). Then submit a ticket to rchelp@rc.fas.harvard.edu that (1) includes the path to the project to add (e.g. /n/piname_project_l3), (2) states that the PI attests Globus is compliant with any contracts/DUAs governing the data, and (3) is either sent by the PI or receives a direct reply from the PI confirming it.

Question 56: question_56

❓ Question

How much CPU and RAM am I actually allowed on the FASRC login server, and is there a cap on how many terminal windows I can have open at once?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/running-jobs, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/R/Large_Data_Processing_R/parallel_computation/R_parLapply_vdi.md, slurm.schedmd.com/srun.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, slurm.schedmd.com/faq.html, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/containers, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/fairshare

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the FASRC documentation: **CPU and RAM limits on login nodes:** Each logged-in account is limited to **1 core and 8GB of memory** per session, enforced by a system-level mechanism called cgroups on the Rocky 8 login nodes. If you exceed these limits, the process will be killed without warning [Cluster Customs and Responsibilities](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities). **Terminal session limit:** You are limited to a **maximum of 5 sessions** per user. If you need more than 5 sessions, consider: - Submitting batch jobs to the cluster instead of using interactive sessions - Using Open OnDemand, which gives you a traditional desktop on a dedicated compute node with the ability to open multiple terminals - Using tools like `screen` or `tmux` to expand one session into multiple sub-sessions [Command line access with Terminal (login nodes)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access) Users who abuse the login nodes may have their login sessions terminated. The login nodes are rebooted monthly as part of maintenance to clear out stale sessions.
✅ Expected Answer
Each login-node session is limited to 1 core and 8GB of memory, and you may have at most 5 sessions per user. Users who abuse the login nodes may have their sessions terminated, and the login nodes are rebooted during monthly maintenance to clear out stale sessions. If you need more than 5 sessions, switch to batch jobs, use Open OnDemand (which gives you a desktop with multiple terminals on a dedicated compute node), or use screen/tmux to multiplex one session.

Question 57: question_57

❓ Question

I ran ssh-copy-id and my key works great when I'm in the office, but from my apartment it still makes me punch in the 6-digit code every single time. Did the key not install properly?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-to-a-compute-node, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/ssh-key-error, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rocky-8-transition-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/git-and-github, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-and-login, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/duo-mobile

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Your SSH key likely installed correctly — the 6-digit code you're seeing is **not** from SSH key authentication, but from FASRC's **two-factor authentication (2FA)** system called **OpenAuth**. Here's what's happening: - **On-campus (office):** You may be on the Harvard network, which can sometimes bypass or simplify the 2FA step, or you may have an active OpenAuth session token. - **Off-campus (apartment):** FASRC requires **both** SSH key authentication **and** OpenAuth 2FA for all remote connections. The 6-digit code is from OpenAuth, not your SSH key. ### What you need to do: 1. **Install and connect to the FASRC VPN** — Off-campus access to cluster resources requires a VPN connection [Access and Login – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-and-login/). 2. **Set up OpenAuth** — If you haven't already, you need to configure the OpenAuth tool for two-factor authentication [Access and Login – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/access-and-login/). See the [OpenAuth Guide](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/openauth/) for setup instructions. 3. **Use the VPN + OpenAuth together** — When connecting from off-campus, you'll need to: - Connect to the FASRC VPN - Use SSH with your key (which works) - Enter the 6-digit code from OpenAuth when prompted Your SSH key is working fine — the 2FA prompt is expected behavior for off-campus access. If you're still having trouble after setting up OpenAuth and the VPN, check the [OpenAuth troubleshooting page](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/troubleshooting/).
✅ Expected Answer
The key is fine — this is by design. Passwordless (ssh-key) authentication is only permitted to the Cannon login nodes from Harvard networks, including Harvard/FASRC VPNs. If you connect from an external network you are still required to provide two-factor authentication regardless of your key. So either connect to the VPN from home, or accept the 2FA prompt. (On FASSE, passwordless ssh is permitted from the FASSE VPN only.) You can confirm the key installed by checking that it is present in ~/.ssh/authorized_keys on the cluster.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/terminal-access

Question 58: question_58

❓ Question

I'm an IQSS researcher and my survey data is classified Level 2. Which cluster am I supposed to be on, and what exactly goes in the VPN box versus the cluster login box?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

For more details on Cannon access, see the [FASRC Cannon cluster documentation](https://www.rc.fas.harvard.edu/services/cluster-computing/#Cluster_Name_Cannon).

Level 2 data belongs on Cannon, the FAS research computing cluster for Data Security Level 2 (only Level 3 data must move to the FASSE secure environment). To get on: in the Cisco AnyConnect VPN client use server address vpn.rc.fas.harvard.edu and enter your FASRC username in the format username@fasrc, your FASRC password, and your FASRC 2FA token — note this is NOT the code associated with your HarvardKey account. Then log into the Cannon cluster at https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu using just your FASRC username WITHOUT the '@fasrc', plus your FASRC password.

Question 59: question_59

❓ Question

My pandas script keeps blowing up with a memory error because the file is bigger than the node's RAM. Does FASRC want me to rewrite it, or can I keep using pandas somehow?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Retrieved Documents: github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Python/Example2/run.sbatch, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise1/Python/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
You don’t need to abandon pandas entirely — you just need to process the file in **chunks** so it fits in memory. ### Quick fix: use `chunksize` with `pd.read_csv` ```python import pandas as pd chunk_iter = pd.read_csv("big_file.csv", chunksize=100_000) # adjust as needed results = [] for chunk in chunk_iter: # process each chunk (filter, aggregate, etc.) results.append(chunk.some_operation()) final = pd.concat(results) ``` Key points: - `chunksize` tells pandas to read only that many rows at a time, keeping memory usage bounded. - Adjust `chunksize` based on your node’s RAM and the row size of your data. - If you’re doing aggregations (e.g., `groupby`), accumulate partial results per chunk and combine them at the end. ### Also check your SLURM memory request If you’re requesting too little memory, you’ll hit SLURM’s `OUT_OF_MEMORY` limit. Make sure your job script requests enough RAM for at least one chunk plus overhead: ```bash #SBATCH --mem=10G # or higher, depending on your data ``` See [Slurm memory limits](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/slurm-memory-limits/) for more on how SLURM enforces memory caps. ### When pandas isn’t enough If your dataset is **much** larger than a single node’s RAM, consider: - **Dask DataFrame** — pandas-like API that works out-of-core and in parallel. - **Polars** — a fast, memory-efficient DataFrame library. - **Spark** (via PySpark) — for distributed processing across multiple nodes. Would you like help estimating a good `chunksize` for your data, or guidance on switching to Dask/Polars?
✅ Expected Answer
Both options exist, and which one FASRC recommends depends on scale. Start with chunking. For moderately large datasets, Dask can enhance Pandas by parallelizing operations — you can convert a Pandas DataFrame to a Dask DataFrame with dd.from_pandas() and keep most of your existing code. But for very large datasets that exceed memory constraints, using Dask alone as a substitute for Pandas is the more effective solution: Dask is a drop-in replacement with a familiar DataFrame API that works lazily, breaking work into smaller tasks that only execute when you call .compute(). Separately, switching from CSV to a columnar format like Parquet (e.g. df.to_parquet('output_data.parquet', compression='snappy')) greatly improves I/O performance.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/python

Question 60: question_60

❓ Question

I switched to the newest Intel module on the cluster and now the compiler command I've always used doesn't exist anymore. What am I supposed to call to build my C file now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Libraries/Armadillo/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
It sounds like you've loaded the newer **Intel OneAPI** module, which replaced the older `icc` command with **`icx`** (the Intel C compiler based on the LLVM/Clang backend). From the documentation, when you load the new Intel module (e.g., `intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`), the compiler binary is located at: ``` /n/sw/intel-oneapi-2023/compiler/2023.0.0/linux/bin/intel64/icx ``` So instead of: ```bash icc my_file.c -o my_program ``` You should now use: ```bash icx my_file.c -o my_program ``` The `icx` command is Intel's new C compiler for the OneAPI toolkit. If you need to check what compiler commands are available after loading a module, you can run `which icx` or `which icc` to see the full path. Would you like help with any specific compilation flags or options?
✅ Expected Answer
Intel renamed the compiler binaries at version 24.0.0. For Intel modules below 24.0.0 the commands are icc (C), ifortran (Fortran), and icpc (C++). For Intel 24.0.0 and above they are icx (C), ifx (Fortran), and icpx (C++). So with a newer Intel module you must invoke icx instead of icc. (You can list the available Intel compilers with `module spider intel` and load a specific one, e.g. `module load intel/23.0.0-fasrc01`.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 61: question_61

❓ Question

Is it fine to build my C code right after I ssh in, and should I just put the gcc line at the top of my job script so it's always fresh?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cpp-programming-language, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/centos7-singularity, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/README.md

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
**No to both.** Here's why: 1. **Don't compile on the login node.** FASRC recommends compiling on a compute node instead, because compilation can take seconds to a minute depending on complexity, and you should use an interactive job (e.g., `salloc`) or the `test` partition for compilation. Compiling on the login node consumes resources that should be reserved for interactive work and job submission. 2. **Don't put the `gcc` (or `icc`) compile command in your `sbatch` script.** The best practice is to compile separately and then use the generated executable in your production batch job. If you include the compile command in the `sbatch` script, it will **recompile every time you submit the job**, which wastes time and resources. **Correct workflow:** - Compile separately on a compute node (using `salloc` or the `test` partition): ```bash module load gcc/9.5.0-fasrc01 gcc -O2 -o myprogram myprogram.c ``` - Then submit your production run as a batch job using the pre-compiled executable: ```bash sbatch myjob.sh ``` - If you change the source code, recompile separately again, then submit the batch job. See the [C Programming Language](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language/) page for full details.
✅ Expected Answer
No on both counts. FASRC recommends jumping to a compute node to compile a C program (compilation can take a few seconds to a minute depending on the code's complexity), and specifically recommends using the `test` partition to compile and test the program before running the production job. Best practice is to compile the code separately and then use the resulting executable in your sbatch script — avoid putting the compilation command inside the sbatch script, because that recompiles the program on every submission. If you change the source, recompile separately and then resubmit the production batch job.

  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/c-programming-language

Question 62: question_62

❓ Question

I want to completely wipe my Julia install off the cluster and start clean. Is there a single command, and does it leave junk behind in my home directory?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Languages/Julia/Notebook.md, slurm.schedmd.com/sacctmgr.html

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
There isn't a single "uninstall Julia" command documented in the FASRC knowledge base. The official installation method uses `juliaup`, which installs Julia into your `$HOME` directory and adds initialization lines to your `~/.bashrc` [Julia Programming Language – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia/). To completely remove it, you'll need to do two things manually: 1. **Remove the Julia installation directory** — `juliaup` typically installs into `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup`. You can remove these with: ```bash rm -rf ~/.julia ~/.juliaup ``` 2. **Clean up `~/.bashrc`** — The installer adds a `juliaup` initialization block to your `~/.bashrc`. You'll need to edit `~/.bashrc` and remove the lines that source `juliaup` (usually something like `eval "$(juliaup shellinit bash)"` or similar). **Does it leave junk behind?** Yes, if you only remove the executable or the `~/.julia` directory but don't clean up `~/.bashrc`, Julia will still appear in your PATH and cause errors. Additionally, Julia packages you installed via `Pkg.add()` are stored in `~/.julia`, so removing that directory will also wipe all your installed packages. If you want to be thorough, you can also check for any Julia-related environment variables or shell functions that might have been added.
✅ Expected Answer
Run `juliaup self uninstall`. That is the uninstall command, but it does not always clean everything up: afterward, check for the `~/.julia` and `~/.juliaup` directories and delete them manually if they are still there. (This matters because the default Julia install location is $HOME, and the installer also adds Juliaup initialization to your ~/.bashrc to put `julia` on your $PATH.)
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/julia

Question 63: question_63

❓ Question

I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/unix-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acls-facls, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-ownership-and-access-policy, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/faq

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The most likely reason your collaborator still gets **Permission denied** despite `chmod 777` is that **POSIX ACLs (Access Control Lists)** are overriding the standard permission bits. ### Why `chmod 777` isn't enough On FASRC storage, files can have **Extended ACLs** attached to them. When an ACL is present, the traditional "owner/group/other" permission bits shown by `ls -l` (and set by `chmod`) are no longer the final authority — the ACL **mask** and explicit user/group entries take precedence. You can check if ACLs are in play by running: ```bash getfacl your_file ``` If you see lines like `user:otheruser:rwx` or a `mask::` entry, ACLs are active. In that case, `chmod` alone won't grant access to a specific collaborator — you need to use `setfacl` to explicitly grant them access. ### How to grant access properly To give a specific collaborator read (or read/write) access, use: ```bash setfacl -m u:collaborator_username:r your_file ``` You can verify the ACL was applied with: ```bash getfacl your_file ``` ### A note on shares - **Tier1 Isilon shares**: ACLs are **not currently supported** on these shares. If you're on an Isilon share, `chmod` should work as expected, and the issue may lie elsewhere (e.g., the collaborator isn't in the right group, or the directory's execute/search permission is missing). - **Other shares**: ACLs may be in effect, and `setfacl` is the correct tool for per-user access. ### Quick diagnostic steps 1. Run `ls -ld your_file` — if you see a `+` at the end of the permission string (e.g., `-rw-r--r--+`), ACLs are present. 2. Run `getfacl your_file` to see the full ACL. 3. If ACLs are present, use `setfacl -m u:collaborator_username:r your_file` to grant access. 4. If you're on an Isilon share and ACLs aren't the issue, double-check that the collaborator is in the correct group and that parent directories have execute (`x`) permission so they can traverse into the folder. For more details, see [Managing file access with ACLs](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/acls-facls/).
✅ Expected Answer
Making the file world-readable is not enough on its own. For a user in the 'Other' category to reach a file, they must first be able to traverse into the top-level share/directory that contains it. If the top level of the lab directory or share is set drwxr-x---, drwxrwx---, or drwxr-s--- then Other has no access to the share at all, even if a file inside it is set -rwxrwxrwx. Other must first be able to 'see' into the directory/share; otherwise it has no access. (FASSE/L3 shares should never allow Other access.) To share with someone outside the group, the enclosing directory must permit Other traversal - e.g. the 'Everyone' directory that lab shares provide for exactly this purpose - or the person can be added to your lab Group by your PI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions
💬 Agent Messages (4)

Args: I chmod 777'd a file in my lab's share so a collaborator in a different lab group could grab it, but they still get permission denied when they try to read it. What's going on?
🛠️ Tool Call #2: search_vectorstore_hybrid
Args: POSIX ACLs access control lists permission denied even with chmod 777

Question 64: question_64

❓ Question

My labmate keeps his personal scratch folder under Users/ in our old lab share, but our newly created share and netscratch have no Users folder. Where am I supposed to put my own personal directory now?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/policy-scratch, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/cluster-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vscode-remote-development-via-ssh-or-tunnel, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/podman

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
On FASRC, **there is no longer a `Users/` folder** inside lab shares or netscratch. The old structure (where each lab member had a `Users/<username>` subdirectory) has been deprecated. ### Where to put your personal files now 1. **For personal, non-shared work:** Use your **home directory** (`$HOME` or `~`), which is automatically created for every user and is private to you. - Path: `/home/<username>` (on the cluster) or accessible via `~` on login nodes. - This is backed up and has a quota. 2. **For shared lab work:** Use the **Lab directory** on your share (e.g., `/n/acc_lab/<labname>/Lab/`). This is the primary working directory shared by all lab members. 3. **For temporary scratch space:** Use **netscratch** (`/n/netscratch`). You should create your own subdirectory directly under `/n/netscratch/<labname>/` (or similar, depending on your lab's setup). Netscratch is **not backed up** and files older than 90 days may be purged. ### Summary | Purpose | Location | |---|---| | Personal files | `$HOME` (`/home/<username>`) | | Shared lab work | `/n/acc_lab/<labname>/Lab/` | | Temporary scratch | `/n/netscratch/<labname>/` (create your own subfolder) | Your labmate's `Users/` folder is from an older convention. You should create your own personal directory under your lab's netscratch path (e.g., `/n/netscratch/<labname>/<your_username>/`) if you need a personal scratch space, or use your home directory for personal files.
✅ Expected Answer
The Users directory only exists on shares created prior to October 2024. On new shares and on /n/netscratch there is no Users directory - FASRC says to use the Lab directory to create personal folders instead. The Lab directory is set drwxrws--- so that every member of the lab group has read/write/execute access to share data amongst the group, and it is the preferred place both for personal folders on new shares and for sharing data with your lab group. On the older shares, the sub-directories in the Users directory are owned by individuals and by default only allow execute access to the group.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions

❓ Question

I finished the cluster account request form and it never asked me to choose a password. How do I actually get a password to log in?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

❌ Status: INCORRECT

You set your FASRC password **after** your account is approved, not during the request form. Here's how: 1. Go to the password reset portal: **[https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/p3/pwreset/](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/p3/pwreset/)** 2. Enter the email address you used to sign up and click **Submit** 3. Check your email for a one-time link and click it 4. Enter a new password on the form that appears **Password requirements:** - At least 12 characters - Both uppercase and lowercase letters - A number and a special character (`!@#$%^&*`) - Must not contain a portion of your name See the full instructions at **[Reset Password](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/reset-password/)**.
✅ Expected Answer
Passwords are not collected by the signup application. Your account is created with a random password, and upon completion of the request you use the password reset tool to set your initial password. You will receive an email once the account has been created with links and instructions to do so.

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, here are the steps you can take to troubleshoot a spinning FASRC portal: 1. **Try a different browser (specifically Firefox):** The documentation notes that **Firefox works best** for FASRC web tools, while Chrome and other browsers sometimes cause errors [How do I get a Research Computing account?](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/how-do-i-get-a-research-computing-account/). If you are using Chrome, try switching to Firefox. 2. **Clear your browser cache and cookies:** A spinning portal after login is often caused by stale session data. Clear your browser's cache and cookies for the portal domain, then try logging in again. 3. **Check your VPN connection:** If you are trying to access the FASSE portal or certain cluster resources, you must be connected to the FASRC VPN [Virtual Desktop](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop/). Ensure your VPN is active and connected to the correct realm (e.g., `@fasrc` or `@fasse`). 4. **Verify your account status:** Ensure your account is active and you have the necessary group memberships. You can check your grant/group status by logging into the [Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/grants) and viewing "My Grants." If these steps do not resolve the issue, you can submit a ticket to FASRC support via the [Portal](https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/rcrt/tickets/).
✅ Expected Answer
If the browser hangs for a long time after authenticating without displaying the Open OnDemand dashboard, restart your Per-User NGINX (PUN) process by visiting: - Cannon: https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ - FASSE: https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/nginx/stop?redir=/pun/sys/dashboard/ Also note that the very first login can legitimately take some time while OOD sets up files and cache in your home directory; that Avast security software can cause 'no internet', 'read error', and 'the requested URL could not be retrieved' messages (disable Web Shield); and that Chrome or Firefox are recommended over Safari and Internet Explorer.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/virtual-desktop

Question 67: question_67

❓ Question

My R job asks Slurm for 8 cores, but when it runs my parallel code spawns dozens of workers and the node grinds to a halt. How am I supposed to determine the worker count inside the script?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/job-defense-shield, slurm.schedmd.com/salloc.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/EP/Example1/README.md, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, slurm.schedmd.com/sbatch.html, slurm.schedmd.com/cpu_management.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise6/train_mnist_ddp.py, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Distributed_GPU_Computing/Exercise5/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/OpenMP/Example2/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise3/R/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Training/Advanced_Cluster_Usage/Exercise2/Cpp/README.md

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The core issue is that your R script is spawning more workers than the number of CPUs Slurm allocated to your job. To fix this, you need to **read the number of CPUs Slurm assigned to your task** and pass that value into your parallel backend. ### How to detect the core count in your R script Slurm sets environment variables that your job can read. The most relevant ones are: * **`SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`** — The number of CPUs allocated to your specific task (this is usually what you want for `--ntasks=1 --cpus-per-task=8`). * **`SLURM_NTASKS`** — The total number of tasks (processes) allocated. You can read these directly in R using `Sys.getenv()`: ```r # Read the number of CPUs allocated to this task ncores <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK")) # Fallback to 1 if the variable isn't set (e.g., running locally) if (is.na(ncores) || ncores == 0) ncores <- 1 ``` ### How to use it in your parallel code Once you have `ncores`, pass it to your parallel backend. For example, if you're using the `parallel` package: ```r library(parallel) # Use the detected core count nworkers <- as.integer(Sys.getenv("SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK", "1")) # Create a cluster with exactly that many workers cl <- makeCluster(nworkers) ``` ### Why this matters If you hardcode a large number of workers (e.g., `makeCluster(32)`), your job will try to use 32 threads/processes even if Slurm only gave you 8 CPUs. This causes **oversubscription**, where threads compete for the same CPU cores, leading to the "grinding to a halt" behavior you described. By reading `SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK`, your script scales correctly with whatever resources Slurm allocates. ### Example SLURM script ```bash #!/bin/bash #SBATCH --job-name=R_parallel #SBATCH --ntasks=1 #SBATCH --cpus-per-task=8 #SBATCH --time=01:00:00 # Export the variable so R can see it export SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK Rscript my_script.R ``` This approach ensures your R job uses exactly the resources it asked for, no more and no less.
✅ Expected Answer
Do not use parallel::detectCores() to determine the number of CPUs/processes to use in your job - it detects the number of physical CPU cores on the compute node, not the number allocated to your job, which is why you end up oversubscribed. Use parallelly::availableCores() instead, which returns the number of CPUs allocated to your Slurm job.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-parallel

Question 68: question_68

❓ Question

I had openmpi loaded, then loaded mpich later in the same session, and now openmpi is gone from my module list. Did I corrupt my environment, and can I keep both around?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example4/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example2/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example3/build.sh, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Courses/CS205/MPI_2021/Example5/build.sh

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
No, you haven't corrupted your environment. This is expected behavior. On the FASRC cluster, **MPI implementations are mutually exclusive** — loading one MPI module (like `mpich`) will automatically unload any previously loaded MPI module (like `openmpi`). This is by design to prevent conflicts between different MPI libraries in the same session. **You can keep both available** — they're just not loaded simultaneously. To switch back to OpenMPI, simply run: ```bash module load openmpi/<version> ``` For example: ```bash module load openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01 ``` You can check what MPI modules are available with: ```bash module spider openmpi module spider mpich ``` For more details, see the FASRC documentation on [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/).
✅ Expected Answer
Nothing is corrupted - this is intentional. The modules are set up so that you can only have one MPI module loaded at a time; if you try loading a second one it will automatically unload the first, which is done to avoid dependency collisions. So you cannot keep both loaded: pick one implementation (OpenMPI or MPICH, each built with either the GNU or the Intel compiler suite) and load it with its matching compiler module, e.g. module load gcc/13.2.0-fasrc01 openmpi/5.0.2-fasrc01. Note also that there is no default MPI library in your environment when you log in - you must load one.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 69: question_69

❓ Question

I need mpi4py in a conda environment, but it has to be built against the same OpenMPI my Fortran code uses. I just ran mamba create with mpi4py in the package list - what MPI did that actually give me, and how do I get OpenMPI instead?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface, slurm.schedmd.com/mpi_guide.html, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/Example1/README.md, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Parallel_Computing/Python/mpi4py/README.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
When you ran `mamba create ... mpi4py` without specifying an MPI implementation, **conda installed MPICH by default** as the MPI library that `mpi4py` links against [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/). If your Fortran code uses **OpenMPI**, you need to rebuild `mpi4py` against that same OpenMPI. Here's how: 1. **Load the compiler and OpenMPI modules** (use the same versions your Fortran code uses): ```bash module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01 module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03 ``` 2. **Load Python** (e.g., `python/3.10.12-fasrc01`) and **create a fresh conda environment** (without `mpi4py` in the initial package list): ```bash mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel source activate python3_env2 ``` 3. **Install `mpi4py` via `pip`** (so it picks up the loaded OpenMPI): ```bash pip install mpi4py ``` This approach ensures `mpi4py` is compiled against the OpenMPI instance provided by the loaded module, matching what your Fortran code uses. For more details, see the FASRC MPI documentation [MPI (Message Passing Interface) & OpenMPI – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface/).
✅ Expected Answer
Putting mpi4py in the mamba create package list (e.g. mamba create -n python3_env1 python numpy pip wheel mpi4py) also installs an MPI library required by mpi4py, and by default that is MPICH - not OpenMPI. If your workflow requires a specific flavor and/or version of MPI, install mpi4py with pip into a custom conda environment instead: load the compiler and MPI modules first (module load gcc/12.2.0-fasrc01; module load openmpi/4.1.5-fasrc03), load a Python module (module load python/3.10.12-fasrc01), create the environment WITHOUT mpi4py (mamba create -n python3_env2 python numpy pip wheel), activate it (source activate python3_env2), then run pip install mpi4py so it builds against the loaded OpenMPI.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mpi-message-passing-interface

Question 70: question_70

❓ Question

I used the 'copy mount path to clipboard' button in Starfish and got /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab, but when I cd to it on a login node I get 'No such file or directory'. What's the correct path?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/starfish-data-management, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/mounting-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/checking-quota-and-usage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The path `/n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab` is the **network mount path** used by Starfish for SMB/CIFS access — it is **not** directly accessible from the FASRC login or compute nodes. On FASRC login or compute nodes, the correct path to your lab directory is: ``` /n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` The difference is: - **`/n/vast-holylabs/...`** — the SMB mount path for desktop/laptop access via Starfish - **`/n/holylabs/...`** — the path used on FASRC login and compute nodes So from a login node, use: ```bash cd /n/holylabs/C/jharvard_lab ``` This is consistent with the FASRC storage documentation, which lists `/n/holylabs` as the path for lab directories [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
The path Starfish copies is not directly usable - you must make two edits: remove 'vast-' and remove '/C'. So /n/vast-holylabs/C/jharvard_lab becomes /n/holylabs/jharvard_lab. The letter C indicates the filesystem is on the Cannon cluster; a group share on FASSE would show F instead of C before the group name.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Question 71: question_71

❓ Question

I want to drag a few files out of my home directory into my lab's netscratch folder using a point-and-click interface, but my home directory doesn't show up as an endpoint in Globus. What else can I use?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/iqss-cannon-quickstart-guide

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Since your home directory isn't exposed through Globus, you can use the **Open OnDemand Remote Desktop** to get a graphical file manager that can see both your home directory and your lab's storage. Here's how: 1. Go to the Open OnDemand dashboard: - Cannon: [https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard](https://rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard) - FASSE: [https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard](https://fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu/pun/sys/dashboard) 2. Start a **Remote Desktop** session ([Remote Desktop documentation](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/vdi-apps/#Remote_Desktop)). 3. Once the desktop starts, open a **File Manager** (click the "Home" folder on the desktop, or go to **Applications → File Manager**). 4. Open a **second File Manager window**. 5. In one window, navigate to your **home directory** (the source). 6. In the other window, navigate to your lab's **netscratch folder** (the destination). 7. Drag and drop files between the two windows. This gives you a point-and-click interface for moving files between home and netscratch. For more details, see [Getting Started with FASRC Storage](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/getting-started-with-fasrc-storage/).
✅ Expected Answer
Globus does not have access to home directories, which is why yours does not appear. For transfers to or from your home directory, use an Open OnDemand Remote Desktop session instead: go to the OOD dashboard (Cannon: rcood.rc.fas.harvard.edu, FASSE: fasseood.rc.fas.harvard.edu), start a Remote Desktop session, open a File Manager by clicking the 'Home' folder on the desktop (or Applications -> File Manager), open a second File Manager window, navigate one to the source (e.g. /n/home01/jharvard/) and the other to the destination (e.g. /n/netscratch/jharvard_lab/Lab/), then right-click the source file -> Copy and right-click the destination window -> Paste (drag-and-drop works too, but behaves like cut-and-paste). Command-line tools (cp, mv, rsync, fpsync) are the alternative; Globus is only recommended for transfers between different filesystems, not within one.

Question 72: question_72

❓ Question

My collaborator is at another university and doesn't have a HarvardKey. Can she still get an account on the cluster, and is there anything I have to keep doing so her access doesn't get shut off?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/onboarding, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/responsibilities, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-signup

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Yes, your collaborator can get an account even without a HarvardKey. According to FASRC's account qualifications: - **If she is a faculty member at another university** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should choose **EXTERNAL** when requesting an account. - **If she is a student or researcher at another university or company** collaborating with a Harvard PI, she should also choose **EXTERNAL**. However, there are important requirements to keep in mind: 1. **Her access must be authorized by the Harvard PI** (you) who sponsors her account. 2. **She must agree to Harvard's policies**, including the Electronic Access and Information Security policies and university research policies. 3. **She will be subscribed to FASRC notifications mailing list**, which is required for all users. 4. **Account access is not permanent** — cluster access is intended for legitimate purposes benefiting Harvard research and must be authorized by the faculty/management of FAS and Research Computing staff. As the sponsoring PI, you are responsible for ensuring her access remains appropriate for the research project. If the collaboration ends or her access is no longer needed, you should request that her account be deactivated. There's no specific "keep doing" action beyond maintaining the legitimate research relationship that justifies her access. For more details, see [Account Qualifications and Affiliations](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/account-qualifications/).
✅ Expected Answer
Yes. A collaborator without a HarvardKey can apply for an external FASRC account through the account request portal (https://portal.rc.fas.harvard.edu/request/account). External accounts have to be re-enabled every 90 days: the PI must request an extension every 90 days or the account will be suspended. Faculty can sponsor FASRC accounts for anyone working in their lab, including external collaborators.

Question 75: question_75

❓ Question

I put a dataset in the Everyone folder so another lab could grab it, but it never shows up in Globus. Is Globus broken?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/storage-service-center

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/globus-file-transfer, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/understanding-permissions, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/tape-globus-access

❌ Status: INCORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Globus isn't broken—**the Everyone folder is not available on Globus**. According to FASRC documentation, the Everyone directory is "intended only for internal sharing" and is explicitly **not** exposed via Globus [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). Globus only exposes the **Lab** directory, which is the primary working directory shared out via Globus [Data Storage (Offerings, Workflow, Costs)](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/data-storage-workflow-rdm/). **What to do instead:** - If you want another lab to access your data via Globus, move or copy the dataset into your **Lab** directory instead. - If the other lab is on the FASRC cluster (has an FASRC account), they can access the Everyone folder directly via SSH/terminal without needing Globus. Let me know if you need help moving the data or setting up Globus sharing from your Lab directory!
✅ Expected Answer
No, that is expected behavior. The Everyone subdirectory is visible to anyone on the HPC cluster and is intended only for internal sharing with other labs on the cluster — it is not available on Globus. The Lab subdirectory is the one shared out via Globus, so data that has to move over Globus needs to live under Lab.

Question 76: question_76

❓ Question

Is there already a samtools container sitting on the cluster somewhere, or do I have to pull one from Docker Hub myself?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG15/shifter.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG15/shifter.pdf, slurm.schedmd.com/SLUG15/shifter.pdf, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/rstudio-server-vs-rstudio-desktop, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-and-rstudio

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Based on the available documentation, there is no pre-installed samtools container on the FASRC cluster. You will need to pull one yourself. The cluster supports SingularityCE, and you have a few options for getting a samtools container: 1. **Pull from Docker Hub:** You can build a Singularity image directly from an OCI container image (like those on Docker Hub) using `singularity build` [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster). 2. **Download from Singularity Library:** You can download an existing Singularity image from the SingularityCE Container Library or other repositories [Singularity – FASRC DOCS](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/singularity-on-the-cluster). If you'd like, I can help you with the specific `singularity build` command to pull a samtools image from Docker Hub.
✅ Expected Answer
There is one already on the cluster. Cluster nodes automount a CernVM-File System at /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/, which provides Singularity images for the BioContainers project (auto-generated from Bioconda packages). Images follow the layout /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/FIRST_LETTER/SECOND_LETTER/PACKAGE_NAME:VERSION--CONDA_BUILD, for example: `singularity exec /cvmfs/singularity.galaxyproject.org/s/a/samtools:1.13--h8c37831_0 samtools --help`. Expect a 10-30 second delay the first time the path is accessed on a compute node that has not mounted/cached it.

Question 77: question_77

❓ Question

A spack install blew up and by the time I looked the build log was already gone. How do I stop spack from throwing the logs away?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/augustus-install.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
By default, Spack stores build logs in a temporary `stage` directory (usually under `/tmp`) and **deletes it after the build finishes**, even if the build fails. To keep those logs for debugging, do two things: 1. **Redirect the stage directory to a persistent location** by setting the `$TMP` environment variable to a path on a disk that won't be cleaned up: ```bash export TMP=/path/to/your/persistent/dir ``` 2. **Use the `--keep-stage` flag** when installing: ```bash spack install --keep-stage <package> ``` This tells Spack to preserve the staging files (including build logs) instead of removing them. You can then inspect the logs in the stage directory under your `$TMP` path. See: [SPACK Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack/)
✅ Expected Answer
Spack builds in a `stage` directory located in /tmp and cleans that space up once it finishes building, whether the build succeeded or failed — which is why the logs disappear. To keep them, first set the `$TMP` environment variable to a location you want the stage files dumped into, then add the `--keep-stage` flag, e.g. `spack install --keep-stage <package>`, which tells spack to keep the staging files rather than remove them.
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Question 78: question_78

❓ Question

Software I built with spack runs fine on the node I built it on but won't run on nodes in other partitions. How do I build something that works everywhere on the cluster?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, github.com/fasrc/User_Codes/blob/master/Notes/augustus-install.md, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/software, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/r-and-rstudio

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
The issue you're experiencing is likely due to Spack's default behavior of autodetecting your hardware's architecture and building software to match it specifically. When you build on one node type, the resulting binaries may only work on nodes with identical or compatible hardware. To build software that works everywhere on the cluster, you need to configure Spack to use a more generic target architecture. Here's how: 1. **Edit your Spack configuration file** (`etc/spack/packages.yaml` in your Spack installation directory). If this file doesn't exist, create it. 2. **Add a generic target architecture** by including the following: ```yaml packages: all: target: [x86_64] ``` This tells Spack to build packages for the generic x86_64 architecture rather than the specific CPU microarchitecture of the node you're building on [SPACK Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack). 3. **Rebuild your packages** with this new configuration. Since Spack hardcodes absolute paths into installed packages, you'll need to rebuild after changing this setting [SPACK Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack). This approach is particularly important when running on heterogeneous hardware where different partitions may have different CPU architectures [SPACK Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack).
✅ Expected Answer
By default Spack autodetects the architecture of the hardware it is building on and optimizes the build for it, so a build made on one CPU generation may not run on another (the docs give the example that LAMMPS built on Sapphire Rapids may not run on Cascade Lake). If you plan to run across heterogeneous hardware/partitions, set Spack to a generic architecture: edit `etc/spack/packages.yaml` inside the spack folder (create it if it does not exist) and add: packages: all: target: [x86_64]
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Question 79: question_79

❓ Question

I don't want spack spending hours compiling its own OpenMPI — how do I make it use the openmpi module we already have on the cluster, and is there anything I have to do before installing?

🎯 Retrieval Check

Expected Document(s): https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack

Retrieved Documents: docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack, docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager

✅ Status: FULLY CORRECT

🤖 archi's Answer
Spack normally builds its own entire dependency stack, but you can tell it to use the cluster's system libraries (including OpenMPI) instead of compiling them from scratch. Here's what to do: ### 1. Discover external libraries Run: ```bash spack external find ``` This scans your environment for libraries and compilers available on the system and registers them as "externals" in your Spack configuration ([Spack Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack)). ### 2. Make sure the OpenMPI module is loaded Before running `spack external find`, load the OpenMPI module you want Spack to use: ```bash module load openmpi ``` This ensures Spack can detect the correct OpenMPI installation path and version. ### 3. Verify Spack sees it After running `spack external find`, check that OpenMPI was detected: ```bash spack find openmpi ``` ### 4. Install your package using the external OpenMPI When installing a package that depends on OpenMPI, Spack should now prefer the external version. You can also explicitly request it in your install spec: ```bash spack install <your-package> ^openmpi ``` ### Before installing — key things to check - **Load the right module** — Spack won't find libraries that aren't in your `$PATH` or module environment. - **Compilers** — If you're using a compiler from a module (e.g., `gcc` or `intel`), you may need to manually add it to Spack's compiler configuration by editing `~/.spack/packages.yaml` and defining it as an external with the correct compiler paths ([Spack Package Manager](https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack-package-manager)). - **Run `spack compiler add`** after loading a module compiler so Spack knows about it. This approach saves you from hours of recompiling OpenMPI and its dependencies.
✅ Expected Answer
Configure the cluster's OpenMPI as an external package in `~/.spack/packages.yaml` (create the file if it does not exist). Get the prefix by loading the modules and reading $MPI_HOME, then add: packages: openmpi: externals: - spec: openmpi@5.0.5%gcc@14.2.0 prefix: /n/sw/helmod-rocky8/apps/Comp/gcc/14.2.0-fasrc01/openmpi/5.0.5-fasrc01 buildable: false The `buildable: false` option ensures MPI won't be built from source and Spack will use the module in that prefix instead. Before installing, run `module purge` — this is required or the build fails (e.g. `module purge` then `spack install hdf5@1.14.6 % gcc@14.2.0 ^ openmpi@5.0.5`).
🎯 Expected Source Documents
  • https://docs.rc.fas.harvard.edu/kb/spack
